ParlaMint-DK_20201119112115	Thank you, President. We are in a situation where the ice melts and the weather gets wilder and the climate changes. We all know. We also know that the government has set itself the wildest goal, and you know it: we need to reduce our CO2 by 2030. But many Danes want to make an active effort on their own. They are trying to live more sustainably and they want to do more. Often I have been asked the question: What can I do for myself? How can I be sure that it will work if I do something? Today we provide the answer to that question, a credible answer: a Climate Forest Fund where you can buy CO 2 But you also get new forest, i.e. such real forest. Because it shouldn't just be some lumber sticks on the ground that stand in straight rows, no, it's real forest with forest brows and variety, and you get low bottom lands for the money. They are taken out of production and flooded, and at the same time you buy space for nature and thus habitats for animals and plants and biodiversity. So the Climate Forest Fund primarily provides climate, but also nature and habitat, and then it provides even more space for outdoor life and picnics. All that the Climate Forest Fund can deliver. The Climate Forest Fund can deliver a credible and documented CO2 I honestly find it difficult to have anything against it. The government, together with the support parties, has already allocated 100 million. kr. In its procurement policy, the Government has decided that state air travel should also be included, and that means that payments will also be made on an ongoing basis. I would also like to stress that we do not need to spend the money on buying land. No, it's the use of the land that you buy. We pay the owners to exploit the soil climate-friendly, stop the normal production, plant trees instead, extract the low bottom soil. That is what we pay for, not the earth itself, and it is forever. So what we are doing today is giving people and businesses back the power of action for the climate. The Climate Forest Fund allows you to do something for yourself, can do something for the climate, can do something for nature, can do it, that there is also more room for those people who love nature and love being out there. Against this background, of course, the Social Democratic Party can only support this proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119114106	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments, and that is why we move on to Mrs Zenia Stamp, Radical Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119114114	Thank you. There is no doubt that forests are part of the solution to the major climate crisis we are facing and a very concrete means of achieving the reduction targets that we ourselves have set ourselves. It is an important means, but it is also important to stress today that it is not the only means, because what is fantastic about the forest as a means is that it absorbs and stores CO2 It is smart, but it is also what makes the forest not solves the task for us alone. But it is an incredibly important instrument and it is very good that we now have a framework for this work with the Climate Forest Fund. But it is also important for us to stress that this is not, of course, the only framework, the only instrument, because what is at the heart of the Climate Forest Fund is voluntaryness. It is that it is an initiative created for the citizens or the authorities, public institutions or companies who want to make a climate contribution, either as a voluntary donation or to compensate for their own emissions, without, however, in a legal framework, but as an opportunity to buy some sort of discharge for the discharge that you might otherwise have. But that is not the only thing, because it should not be a secret today that what we really believe can make a difference is not voluntary, it is regulation, and that is that the polluter pays. Nor should there be any doubt about that today, but we very much welcome all the citizens, public companies and private companies who want to make a voluntary contribution, buy indulgences, so to speak, for the emissions they are responsible for. But, of course, that is not the only solution. So for the foundation design itself. It's been a balancing act. It is incredibly important that there is a great deal of credibility about the reductions that this forest fund is supposed to make. In other words, if a company wants to write in its annual accounts that they have bought some kind of climate compensation, one must also believe that this is actually the reduction that has taken place out there. So it is incredibly important that there is credibility, so we have the certainty when you buy some form of compensation or donate to the climate through a forest fund. And that is why it is not just such a charitable purpose; it is a purpose and a fund that must be run very professionally and with the emphasis that we also get the reductions that are being put in store for the individual investor. Then there has been a lot of focus on the construction • Perhaps not so much the foundation construction itself, but the interaction with other foundations out there in the landscape. And when you read the hearing answers, you can see that they split up roughly fifty-fifty. There are those who believe that it is too clear with a Climate Forest Fund, that is, that there should be more focus on biodiversity and nature, and then on the other hand there are those who say: Well there is too much nature and biodiversity, because there must be a very clear demarcation and focus on climate. And that is why I believe that we are hitting a fairly good balance, where the focus, as is also very clear from the draft law, is climate. However, when we are going to plant a lot of trees for the benefit of the climate, we think it is very good that we are also thinking of synergies and that it can also become more concrete when we, as an investor, have to decide what we really want to buy for • so that we may be able to put down some more money and then buy a product package, which therefore also contains something that benefits biodiversity and nature. We think this is the right balance, but of course we will follow this fund and the interaction with the other funds in the coming years to see if there is the right and necessary demarcation, so that it doesn't just flow together and it becomes completely opaque for the individual consumer or the individual company. We strongly support the idea that we should be able to see on the bottom line what reductions the initiatives lead to. Of course, that is the crucial thing, but it must also be possible to think nature and biodiversity in terms of both the fund, but also for the individual investor, who says which I think is a perfectly legitimate need: I want to compensate for something CO2 That is what we think is sensible.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119122259	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments, and that is why we are now welcoming the Minister for the Environment.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119122307	Thank you. Thank you, too, for your positive reception in this Chamber. Many Danes and companies want to make a difference to the climate, and I think we all think that is enormously positive. However, there has been a lack of a clear possibility which ensures both concrete and credible climate compensation on Danish soil. With the Danish Climate Forest Fund, we provide the opportunity to reduce climate by contributing precisely to Denmark's 70-percent target by 2030. Of course, we must all try first and foremost to lower our CO2 through the Foundation, each of us can help to take good care of the climate through efforts on Danish soil. The Fund operates by receiving, on the one hand, financial contributions from, for example: citizens and companies who receive a receipt for purchased CO2 There has already been a great deal of support for the Danish Climate Forest Fund, both from the green organisations and from the industry in connection with the public consultation. So now I hope that the Danes will also take in the Danish Climate Forest Fund so that we can have better climate, better environment, more nature, more forests and more open air. We are in the midst of a climate crisis and we are in the midst of a natural crisis and both require action. With the Danish Climate Forest Fund, we act on the climate crisis, but at the same time we also act on the natural crisis. And I would like to say, in relation to the debate that has taken place in this Chamber, that the effort for the climate, as the name of the fund also says, is the main purpose of the Danish Climate Forest Fund, but we have made sure to write into the bill that the fund also always works with regard to nature and biodiversity, because we should not save the climate at the expense of nature. Therefore, it has also been important to me that the forests that we travel through the Danish Climate Forest Fund do not become slim plantations without biodiversity • We should not make clean timber factories. On the other hand, it will be forests with broadleaves, conifers and forest brows, so that we can have robust forests with a balanced climate and natural effect. And then it has to be forests that we can travel in that we have also been asked about as usual and which we can enjoy in the countryside. This brings us all joy. The second main activity, which has filled a little less in today's debate, is that for the new fund there will also be the possibility of the removal of low-level soils, and here the emission of CO2 can also be good opportunities for a natural boost. Both the new forests and the extracted low bottom areas are secured by land registration, so they are in the landscape, and we have secured all of this with the bill. It must be forests that benefit both the climate and our nature and the environment. Many have called for the opportunity to contribute financially to concrete climate projects in Denmark. By supporting the fund, the individual citizen or company can contribute to the green conversion, and everyone can contribute the amount they want. There is no upper or lower limit. Specifically, you will be able to support the Danish Climate Forest Fund through donations, contributions and co-financing to the fund, and pilot owners can contribute by making their land available so that the fund can raise forest or take out low bottom land. With the Danish Climate Forest Fund, we finally get a legitimate and credible recipient of voluntary funding in the climate area, and this is something that has been demanded from many sides. I share the experience that has also been described here that we have seen too many examples of how things have gone wrong when people, including companies, have invested in something that has been difficult to understand and where it has proved not to have the effect that they had hoped for. I would like to point out that we have previously established a fund that has nature as its focus, namely the Danish Nature Fund. It has also filled something today. It is true that it is a heartchild, also for a social democratic government, because we were also involved in setting up that fund. With the new Climate Forest Fund, we get a good supplement... that's how we see it. It is important, therefore, that the Climate Forest Fund also coordinate its efforts with other funds and actors, because there must be room for everyone. It is absolutely crucial for the success of the Danish Climate Forest Fund that it is built around credibility and openness. One must therefore be able to rely on it to bear something and that the contributions you make to the fund also make a real difference to the climate. Therefore, the Danish Climate Forest Fund will be established as an independent management unit. A board of directors and an advisory committee are set up to ensure that the fund also meets the obligations laid down in the law; obligations which are primarily aimed at the climate, but which also relate to biodiversity, nature and recreational purposes. The Fund shall continuously calculate, calculate and monitor the climate impact of the supported projects. The inventories will be included in a register published on the Fund's website and then the climate statements will be verified by an independent third party. We've also placed a buffer in the fund. The duration of the projects must be ensured by the Danish Climate Forest Fund allocating a certain proportion of all CO2 2 in connection with forest fires or stormfall. In this way, we will ensure that the fund becomes a credible recipient of contributions from citizens, companies, associations and anyone else who wants to be involved. The climate effect is counted in the Danish climate inventories and thus also contributes to the realisation of the Danish climate objectives and only there. That is to say, there will be no double counting, as the CO2 start-up grant for the fund, as has also been said, was sounded by the Finance Act last year. Thank you to the parties that have helped us now to allocate the first 100 million. kr. to the Climate Forest Fund. We've worked hard to get a bill in place. Indeed, the construction of the fund involves a whole new state organisation in an area which is also characterised by a very high degree of complexity, and we also want the fund's projects and climate accounting to meet the absolutely highest international standards in this area. We have consulted experts in this area, we have held a large number of meetings with stakeholders to also identify the wishes for the establishment, and now we are so ready with the bill, and I am infinitely happy and proud of that. Afforestation and set-aside are simple climate tools that can make a really big difference, while we transform both society and our habits in a greener direction. The low-level interventions have a good effect on the short track, and the fund can choose to start with the smaller areas, which are not so complex, so as to have an even faster climate effect. Afforestation is cheaper than the removal of low-level soils per year. In contrast, as has also been said in the debate, it takes a little longer for the forest to grow up and have a full effect. That is why we have also chosen to be able to do both. We have chosen this because we believe it is important to act both in the short term and in the long term. In another direction, with the new green procurement strategy, we have just ensured that the government will also introduce climate compensation for all state flights via the fund, and in this way we will also ensure beyond the 100 million. kr., which is already in the foundation, that new money can be put in the box for new forest and procurement. If we are to achieve our very, very ambitious climate targets in Denmark, we need to think in new ways. The whole of Denmark has to pull in the same direction, and therefore we also invite with this fund that all Danes can contribute to the climate struggle. With the Danish Climate Forest Fund, the state, companies and citizens are working together to finance the green transition. And with the state in the back, with the Danish Crown, companies and citizens also get security that their contributions make a real difference to the climate, so that together we can achieve what is the whole purpose. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119124843	Thank you. There are no brief comments. Then I understand that it's sir. Stinus Lindgreen, rapporteur for the Radical Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119124852	Thank you. Yes, my colleague was just about to hold some debates, so I was allowed to hold this rapporteur's speech on L 95. The law complements the rules of the EU Market Monitoring Regulation. The new rules include, inter alia: the possibility of obtaining information on software embedded in products, the possibility of performing market surveillance under concealed identity and the possibility of blocking access to an online interface if products presenting a serious risk are placed on the market. The law extends these rules to all chemicals, including those not covered by EU legislation. There should be no difference between the possibility of supervising dangerous products, whether they are covered by the Market Surveillance Regulation or national rules. The draft law also provides for the introduction of a publication system; for the prohibition of products where the supervisory authorities cannot obtain the necessary information; for the taking of samples from Internet shops at their own expense; for seizures; and finally, the draft law empowers the Minister to lay down rules on registration requirements. This is a follow-up to the agreement on a pesticide strategy concluded between the Social Democratic Party, the Radical Left, the Socialist People's Party, the Left, the Liberal Alliance, the Conservative People's Party and the Danish People's Party. The Radical Left therefore supports the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119125448	The Minister for the Environment has put forward a draft law amending the law on chemicals and environmental protection, fertilisers and soil improvers. The new powers include the possibility of obtaining information; the possibility of carrying out market surveillance under concealed identity; the possibility of requiring changes or removal of content on online interfaces; the possibility of blocking access to an online interface if products presenting a serious risk are placed on the market; the power to establish a publication system; the power to prohibit the sale, import and use of substances when the supervisory authorities do not obtain the information they have requested; and the power to carry out seizure to a greater extent than in the past. In addition, the Minister for the Environment is authorised to set up a registration system for manufacturers, suppliers, importers, exporters, dealers, storage containers or distributors of industrial pesticides. It is a comprehensive list of new powers, but the measures seem sensible. It is, of course, positive that the supervisory authorities have the necessary powers for effective supervision so that they can take action against products which pose a serious risk. ParlaMint-DK_20201119125616	Thank you. There are no brief comments, and that is why it is now the Minister for the Environment. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119125622	Thank you for that and also thank you for your positive reception. It may sound a bit technical when you read it, but the legislative proposal that we have to deal with today will strengthen the supervisory authorities' tools and give them better powers to ensure that the legislation is complied with. In this way it is both an important legislative proposal, I think, but also one that I have great expectations of will have a concrete meaning out there. There are three main areas. First of all, the legal basis for a registration system for distributors of pesticides. Secondly, a number of powers for better control of chemicals in products, including in particular: a publication system. Thirdly, a number of powers for effective supervision, particularly with regard to trade on the Internet. With the powers, we are implementing the market monitoring regulation in the environmental field, and I am pleased that there was broad political support for the pesticide strategy that we agreed on in 2017, where the objective of the strategy, among other things. is to reduce the infringement rate in relation to the sale and possession of illegal pesticides. Nowadays, the chemical inspection only controls the companies with which they are familiar, and knowledge is largely based on estimates and experience. The draft law will first of all provide for a requirement for the registration of all those who negotiate these pesticides for professional use in Denmark. The purpose of the registration system is thus to give the chemical inspection a better basis for both planning and targeting its supervision and control, both in terms of frequency and in terms of length of the individual companies. In addition, it will also provide an overview of dealers who can be used to enable the Danish EPA to fulfil the EU legal obligations on reporting. When we know all the links in the chain and the overview is also gathered somewhere, it will also be much easier for the authorities to communicate directly with the dealers and for example. inform them that they must remove funds from the shelves if they have been banned. This could reduce the risk of funds being banned being sold to professional consumers. Secondly, with the legislative proposal, I will ensure that in the field of chemicals we have even better opportunities to monitor and control chemicals in products. This is important because consumers must be able to trust that the products they buy also meet the requirements for chemicals, whether they are products that they physically take down from a shelf in a store or whether they do so on the Internet. And that is also why I propose to set up a publication system which will allow the supervisory authority to publish, for example. which products are illegal and also who the dealer is. It can, for example, be done. be a situation where prams with illegal chemicals have been sold. The scheme will, on the one hand, inform consumers so that they can avoid those products and, on the other hand, benefit those companies which use a lot of resources to ensure that their products comply with the rules, that is to say. aid to all those who actually follow the rules we have decided, but also a whip for those companies that do not. I also propose that we increase the possibility of confiscating illegal products so that fewer products reach Danish consumers. This is necessary because we can see that private trade on the Internet is increasing. The Corona crisis has also increased the need for this change, because we can see an extensive import of illegal biocides in the form of hand alcohol for both private and business operators. In the future, seizures may be carried out in all cases where, in the specific situation, it is the most appropriate response to an infringement, e.g. at the airport, if the product cannot be legalised and that is not possible today. I also propose that the supervisory authority should be able to take samples from online shops at the company's own expense. It will equalise online shops with physical shops, and it will also be easier for the supervisory authority to decide whether a product complies with the rules. And then I propose that the supervisory authority be given the power to issue orders against the sale, import and use of products when companies do not provide the information they have been asked for. This possibility has so far been limited in cases where the company does not disclose the chemical composition of a product, although they have been asked to do so. This has meant that, in relation to all other types of information, the supervisory authority has only had the very resource- and time-consuming opportunity to report to the company because it has not been possible to prohibit, for example, sales. Therefore, the possibility of issuing injunctions and prohibitions now extends to all kinds of information. As the third element, the draft law contains provisions implementing the EU's environmental market surveillance regulation. The aim of the regulation is to ensure uniform and effective market surveillance and control of products throughout the EU. This means, inter alia, that the national authorities must have a number of specific powers. Denmark already carries out sound environmental control, but there is a need to extend the toolbox in a number of ways in order to meet the requirements of the regulation. Among other things: there is a need to adapt the Danish rules to the new digital reality facing supervisory authorities. Firstly, the authorities must be able to obtain all the information necessary to assess whether a product complies with EU legislation. There can be, among other things: need access to the software products, details of the distribution network and information on the ownership of a website. Secondly, our authorities must be able to exercise control without making themselves known, that is. under covert identity. The possibility of checking under hidden identity ensures that the authorities are not given very good products or are told that the product is sold out when the webshop can see that it is the control that buys. Thirdly, the authorities are given the opportunity to require illegal products to be removed from websites, apps and the like, or they may require changes to the content, for example. by requiring a warning to be displayed when the user enters the page. And if a command to remove or change content is not followed, it will be possible to block the online interface completely, for example. the entire website if the products present a serious risk. In this way, consumers are protected from traders who, despite the authorities' instructions, sell or distribute dangerous products. In addition, the draft law goes beyond the requirements of the market surveillance regulation in some areas. On the one hand, we propose that some of the new powers should apply not only when monitoring common EU legislation, but also when monitoring national rules in the Chemicals and Fertilisers Act, on the other hand, it is proposed that online interfaces, for example. webshops can be blocked, not only when a command to remove or change content is not followed, but also in repeat cases. And this is an important step, which I am also pleased to see, among other things. Danish Occupation backs up and will provide better opportunities to stop companies that simply replace an illegal product with another illegal product. So I look forward to following the application of the law in practice, because we have also been discussing with each other for a long time here how to ensure that there is in fact a high level of protection for consumers and the environment, even when there is online trade. I can also only welcome the fact that in this way the Danish People's Party will find an extra joy in EU cooperation when we come forward with this proposal. Against this background, I would like to thank you for all the good statements so that we can ensure better enforcement in this area. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119130614	Thank you very much to the Minister for the Environment. There are no further brief comments to be made to the Minister. ParlaMint-DK_20201119130630	The debate is open. The first rapporteur is from the Social Democratic Party. Sir. Kasper Roug.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119130650	Thank you for that, President. We must take care of our nature, and the state of Danish nature is unfortunately not one that has improved since 2013. It was a bottom-up task to improve the conditions of our common nature. The legislative proposal we are discussing today aims to ensure that the implementation of the 2004 EU Environmental Liability Directive is, of course, correct. In concrete terms, it is proposed that the definition of protected species and habitat types in the environmental liability directive be implemented more closely into legislation, and it is also proposed that the person responsible for environmental damage should always be responsible for preventing and remedying environmental damage. In the legislation as it is now, it is possible, in exceptional circumstances, to exempt a person responsible for environmental damage if, for example, it: has taken place because of negotiations or actions by a third person and where the person responsible has taken the appropriate security measures. So if the person responsible can prove not to be guilty of any environmental damage, it has not been possible to act further. Of course, we're changing that now. The draft law proposes to introduce a duty of action for the person responsible so that it is not possible in future not to act on environmental damage or on the risk of environmental damage. The necessary measures must now be ensured. However, there will be situations where the person responsible does not have to bear the costs in the particular circumstances which have previously been exempted from liability for environmental damage. That is why it is proposed at the same time to set up a compensation scheme so that those responsible for environmental damage, who are exempt from costs, can act and recover the costs that have then been caused by the damage. In addition, the draft law proposes a more directive-by-law implementation of the definition of protected species and habitat types. The environmental liability directive aims to prevent and remedy damage to our nature. This involves damage to our soil, our aquatic environment, protected habitats, protected species and species habitats. With the implementation of this definition, we extend the protection of our nature, so that the environmental damage law will also apply to damage to protected species and their habitats and protected habitats both within and outside the designated Natura 2000 sites without exception. Action must be taken if there is damage to our nature or if there is a danger. This bill therefore strengthens the protection of our nature and helps to ensure that our implementation of the Environmental Liability Directive is close to directive. That is why the Social Democrats support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119131707	Thank you for the floor. The Government has put forward a draft law amending the Environmental Damage Act, the Environmental Protection Act and other laws. ParlaMint-DK_20201119131814	Thank you to the rapporteur from the New Citizens' Party. As I do not see a rapporteur from the Liberal Alliance in the Chamber, I can give the floor to the Minister for the Environment, who has already jumped up here on the platform. Welcome.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119131825	We shall continue with the fast-track approach in this Chamber. The European Commission has announced in a communication to the Danish Government that parts of the Danish implementation of the 2004 Environmental Liability Directive do not comply with the Directive. The government has therefore looked at the Danish rules and, in accordance with the Commission's request, we have decided to amend the Danish implementation of the directive on two points: the rules on the liability of the person responsible in the event of environmental damage and the rules on the damage to protected species and habitats covered by the environmental damage concept. The aim of the draft law is therefore to ensure the correct implementation of the Environmental Liability Directive. At the same time, we ensure that the person responsible for environmental damage, such as: come from a polluting company, in future always have a duty to clean up. This is for the benefit of all of our environments. Environmental damage control is complex and extensive, and I am also aware that there is a desire for regulation to be implemented. In this situation, however, we are merely correcting the criticisms raised by the Commission. Today, the Environmental Liability Directive has been implemented in the Environmental Damage Act, the Environmental Protection Act and several other laws and aims to prevent and remedy future major damage to soil, water and protected species and habitat types, the so-called environmental damage. The rules include the pollution or damage to our nature and aquatic environment, where there is a significant negative impact as a result of the damage, and the contamination on soil which poses a significant risk of human health being adversely affected. The environmental liability rules here ensure that the person responsible for environmental damage immediately takes the necessary preventive measures or, if the environmental damage has occurred, takes any practical steps to remove or otherwise deal with the pollutants or other harmful effects in question immediately. This is done to limit and prevent further environmental damage and adverse effects on human health or further deterioration of exploitation potential. The starting point is that the person responsible for the damage bears the costs of the preventive and remedial measures. The directive is therefore based on a principle, as has also been mentioned earlier today, that the polluter should pay for environmental damage. The rules on environmental damage are therefore the responsibility of those responsible for the operation of an undertaking. The Environmental Liability Directive contains two types of liability: an objective responsibility for particularly polluting activities, in which the person responsible should not have made mistakes in order to be covered, and a culpa-based responsibility for damage to protected species and habitat types where the person responsible should have made a mistake. The rules we are amending here relate only to objective responsibility and could, for example, relate to the discharge of pollutants into the aquatic environment. The starting point here is that you are always responsible and have to pay for repairing the damage. However, according to the rules in force, the person responsible for environmental damage must not, in some cases, take the necessary action if, for example, The damage was caused by someone else. In other words, there is no obligation to start cleaning up. That's what the government is proposing to change. In future, the person responsible for environmental damage will also be obliged, in the specific cases, to take preventive and remedial measures to benefit the environment of all of us. The person responsible for environmental damage will therefore not be able to claim that there are situations where responsibility has been relaxed in relation to the directive. In this way, we ensure that there can no longer be stray environmental damage and that there will always be someone responsible to clean up the environmental damage caused by, for example, environmental damage. polluting activities. In future, however, the person responsible will not have to bear the costs of environmental damage or the imminent danger of environmental damage. For example, the person responsible shall not bear the costs if the damage has been caused by another person. In this context, I am talking about the situations in which the person responsible is not in control of the circumstances which led to the damage to the environment. In such cases, the government proposes that in future the person responsible should be able to recover reasonable costs from the State, and this is in line with the rules of the Environmental Liability Directive. It is important for me to state that it will continue to be the very clear starting point that whoever is responsible for these major damage to our environment and nature, which is an environmental damage, must also in future clean up after themselves and pay for it. At the same time, it is important to establish that, fortunately, we have very few environmental damage cases in Denmark. Since the adoption of the Danish environmental damage problems in 2008 and until now, there has been only one case in 2018 in which the rules on environmental damage were applied. In accordance with the "polluter pays' approach, the undertaking responsible in the specific case itself incurred the cost of remedying. Therefore, there have so far been no cases in which the State would have to bear the costs of remedying it. It must therefore be assumed that the frequency of the cases covered by this bill will be very low. At the same time, we propose to change the definition of protected species and habitat types in the Environmental Damage Act. The amendment means that the definition will be in line with the environmental liability directive. This means that when we talk about environmental damage to protected species and their habitats and habitats, the environmental damage law applies both within and outside the designated Natura 2000 sites without exception. Protection has to a certain extent been limited to Natura 2000 sites, and protection is now extended to habitat types and habitats from Annex II species of the Habitats Directive outside Natura 2000 sites. It is a bit complicated matter, but the government hopes that the Folketing will be able to agree to our proposal for a law amending the environmental damage law and the law on environmental protection and various other laws. I look forward to the committee reading. Thank you for the floor.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119132410	Thank you, Minister. There is a request for a brief comment on the draft law, and that is Mr. Søren Egge Rasmussen, Enhedslisten. Here you go.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119143331	Thank you. As I said earlier, I do not think this is an easy debate or an easy one. The position of the Enhedslisten on boy circumcision is clear: we have a decision from our 2008 annual meeting which clearly states that religious and culturally related surgical interventions are a choice that the individual must make himself. The cutting off of healthy functional tissue is an intervention that cannot be decided by the parents on behalf of their unborn children. On the other hand, we do not believe in the Enhedslisten that the rules on female genital mutilation should be relaxed, which would be a consequence of this citizens' proposal as it is now formulated. That is why we will be able to table an amendment which clarifies or amends the proposal, so that it is only a question of an age limit for the genital mutilation of male children, in order to ensure that the citizens' proposal will still live up to the intentions that we are at least reading out of the debate, but at the same time ensure that we do not relax the rules on female circumcision. In the Enhedslisten, we think it is important that the proposal should be followed by an information campaign aimed at those parents who are in those environments where boy circumcision is either religious or culturally widespread. It is a very good thing if we can have a dialogue with the respective religions about an age limit, but I think that what really needs to be done if we want a change of attitude on the genital mutilation of boys is that we also talk to the parents who are actually dealing with these questions about their children. There is no doubt that this debate on boys' circumcision is sensitive, and it is because two fundamental rights are colliding. On the one hand, there is the child's right to decide for himself about his own body and the need not to expose a child who cannot himself be involved in that decision to unnecessary pain or to an unnecessary risk of complications, and complications are always at risk of surgery. On the other hand, we have the right to practice our faith, that is, freedom of religion. It's relevant in this context, because ritual circumcision is part of both Judaism and Islam. But when we in the Enhedslisten weigh these two considerations against each other, it is the consideration of the child's right to decide over his own body that weighs the heaviest. That is why we can support this citizens' proposal with the amendment that we ourselves will be bringing into the debate in committee. Finally, I would like to say that we have a concern which I think we need to take into account when discussing this citizens' proposal in the citizens' proposal, they themselves also mention a number of different solutions, and that is the level of penalties. It must not be the case that parents, if there is going to be a circumcision against which there is a ban, do not dare to go to the doctor if there are major complications about it. That is why I think it is important that we have a debate afterwards on, if we get so far, that we make an age limit that we then ensure that the level of punishment that might follow is, of course, proportional to the age limit that we are setting. So it was just one last thing I wanted to flag in this debate. However, the Enhedslisten therefore gives the child's right to his own body the highest priority, and we can therefore support the citizen's proposal and, as I said, come with an amendment.

But we certainly do not want to hit free churches and foreign Christian churches that, for example, do not want to hit free churches. do not want to recommend abortion or homosexual marriages. If the law is going to hit that way, it will be a nuisance to some people who should not be bothered. That is why we are a little concerned about whether the draft law is too vague. The very purpose has been expressed very broadly, namely that you should not receive money from the European Parliament and I quote "people who are working against or undermining democracy and fundamental freedoms and human rights'. The best thing would be, of course, if we were to say plainly in advance that what we do not want donations from are countries such as Iran and Saudi Arabia or extreme Islamist organisations, and that they must not be donations to extreme Islamist organisations and mosques in Denmark. This bill clearly shows what the weakness of our open democratic society is, namely that we will give freedom to all and that we are afraid to make a difference. But the problem today is that we cannot expect everyone to support the rules of the game, that is. freedom of mind and democracy. There are some who use freedom of spirit and democracy to undermine and destroy freedom of spirit and democracy. At the same time, our tradition of freedom makes it almost impossible to direct the law to those whom we want to stop because we do not want to make any difference. This is a problem that must be resolved before we seriously undermine ourselves. I am sure that there are many subtle and difficult legal problems in a wording that is directed directly at what we care about: Islamic associations in Denmark, which receive money from Iran and Saudi Arabia or organisations such as the Muslim Brotherhood, without the law becoming a major nuisance to a number of peaceful and law-abiding people. However, I would urge that the further work on the bill should try to guard against them and ensure that the fences are free churches and other Christian churches so that they do not run the risk of being hit. The Danish People's Party supports the purpose of the bill, but we need to be sure of the precision of the bill so that it does not hit these Christian churches I am talking about before we can promise to vote in favour. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119153801	Is it the best of all times or the worst of all times? In fact, I recently read Charles Dickens' book "A tale of two cities', and it struck me that at that time it was the worst of all times in the sense that there was poverty, women's oppression, religious oppression and an undeveloped democracy, but at the same time it is the story of Paris and London at a time in Europe's history where our society is evolving, where our society is marked by the Enlightenment and where the first cautious building blocks for European democracy are laid, and I think Dickens has a look for both. Today, as a Social Democrat, when I am so concerned that we are so insistent on defending Danish democracy, it is also because it is not only because, but also because I believe that the society that has a high degree of equality, a high degree of freedom and a high level of equality can only exist if democracy actively defends the principles on which it itself is based. And I am convinced that there are people in our society who are actively undermining, undermining and fighting precisely those principles, and that we have probably been a little too naive and a little too late to discover how consciously the foundations of our society are quietly being undermined. It is not as bad in Denmark as I think it is in other European countries, but it should not prevent us from taking initiatives which this bill is just a cautious step towards. That is why I would like to start by thanking you for a good debate on the legislative proposal. I also think that the debate here shows that there is, in fact, broad political consensus that the very foundations of our democracy and our constitution and everything that is so fundamental to our society must be defended. In fact, I have not heard a single party on the platform disagree with that. Unfortunately, it is not self-evident that we have the necessary safeguards around our democracy that are needed. I believe that it requires more active action and political will to set a limit when necessary. And I think it is when it comes to donations from anti-democratic forces to, for example. religious institutions in Danish society. It is therefore important for us in the government to take a step towards ending the forces that will undermine our democracy. This is also true in relation to donations, because, as the Conservative rapporteur says and an old phrase says: whoever pays the orchestra can also help pay for the music. That's why we shouldn't be naive. Money and gifts are not necessarily always neutral charity. Overall, therefore, with this bill, the government wants to make it forbidden in Denmark to receive anti-democratic donations. Specifically, the aim of the draft law is to prevent natural and legal persons from working against or undermining democracy and fundamental freedoms and human rights by giving donations. This also applies to foreign government authorities and state-controlled organisations and companies. With the bill, we propose the establishment of a system whereby, on the recommendation of the Foreign Affairs Council, the Foreign Minister can decide on the admission of both natural and legal persons who oppose or undermine democracy and fundamental freedoms and human rights; they must be able to be included on a list of prohibitions. Prior to specific decisions, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs must be consulted on any significant foreign policy implications. If the Ministry of Foreign Affairs considers that the inclusion of the person concerned in the public list of prohibitions could have significant foreign policy implications, the Minister of Foreign Affairs and Integration fails to include this natural or legal person on the list of prohibitions. Otherwise, the natural or legal person will have to be included in the list of prohibitions if the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration considers that the conditions for such inclusion are otherwise fulfilled. In connection with the inclusion of the person on the list, a concrete and individual assessment will have to be made as to whether the person in question is working against or undermining democracy and fundamental freedoms and human rights. It will also be a prerequisite that there is a certain likelihood that the person concerned intends to provide donations to Denmark. The list of prohibitions shall be drawn up for four years and may be renewed for four years at a time. It will be based on a concrete assessment which personal data will be necessary to publish in each case in order to ensure a precise identification of the person concerned. It is proposed that it be prohibited to receive one or more donations which, individually or in combination within 12 consecutive months, exceed DKK 10,000. from a natural or legal person included in the public prohibition list. A violation of the ban would be punishable by a fine. In cases where, only after the date of receipt of a donation from a person on the prohibition list, the recipient becomes aware of receipt, and also at the time of receipt should not have been aware of receipt, the recipient shall return the donation to the donor or, if that is not possible, transfer it to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. Donations in the form of amounts other than sums of money that cannot be returned will have to be collected by the recipient on terms previously approved by the Ministry and an amount equal to the selling price minus any costs associated with the sale would have to be transferred to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. A breach of the obligation to return or transfer the donation may also be punishable by a fine. Enforcement of the ban would have to take place within the general principles of the Code of Procedure, and illegally received donations could be confiscated. Quitting anti-democratic donations is obviously not an easy task, and there will probably be many challenges in completely closing off anti-democratic donations. But it must not stop us from taking action, taking a step and defending the fundamental values of Danish society, and, as the proposed bill shows, the government is proposing that the scheme should be evaluated three years after its entry into force, and we are working after its entry into force on 1 February next year. Then we will have to look at whether it has worked as intended and see where we might be able to improve the system. In conclusion, I would like to stress that this proposal for a donation law is not, of course, alone, but must be seen as one of several steps that the government will take in order to put an end to extremist forces. For example: We are also going to put forward a legislative proposal before Christmas to strengthen efforts to combat negative social control, and we are also looking at whether greater openness can be created about the sermons held in languages other than Danish. In those words, I would like to thank the rapporteurs for a good debate.

The written submission of the bill states: "Danske virksomheder must be able to cope with international competition, and it must therefore be easy and unbureaucratic for Danish companies to recruit highly qualified and skilled foreign employees. ▪ New citizens are so much in favour of an alien policy that ensures that foreigners who do not contribute value to our society are lost or sent out in the event that they are already here. The economic growth and prosperity that they create is, after all, economic growth and prosperity which benefit all of us, and so I have to ask the rapporteur: is it the rapporteur's conviction that this bill really meets what is stated in the introduction to the proposal, precisely that it should be easy and unbureaucratic for Danish companies to get foreign workers to do so, and that this bill promotes the easy and unbureaucratic, or does it really do the opposite, namely make it more bureaucratic and more difficult?

ParlaMint-DK_20201119170828	We thank Mrs Pernille Vermund. Then I shall give the floor to the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119170838	Thank you for a good debate on the bill. When companies in Denmark bring labour to the country, it must, of course, be done on proper pay and working conditions similar to what is normal here in Denmark, because cheating undermines the common rules of the labour market, and it gives itself that it is unacceptable, and I am glad to hear that there is broad support about that too. After all, it is not fair to all those employers who can find a way of complying with the rules, and the employees who do their job, that someone is trying to cheat. At the end of last year, cases were brought up in the media with examples of foreigners and of companies that cheat and bypass the rules we have in foreign law, for example. The catering industry, where there were many who worked for a very low salary, or spouses who worked two for one's price. We have since strengthened control, but unfortunately the examples suggest that some will go a long way to cheat on the rules. That is why we need more tools to assess applications and carry out ex post controls, because although some of the authorities have done so within the current rules, there is clearly a need for more with the stories we have seen. Therefore, with this bill, the government is proposing a series of measures to strengthen the control of foreign labour and the companies employing foreign labour. Firstly, we propose that foreigners who come to Denmark under one of the occupational schemes should have their salary paid in a Danish bank account and that only the salary paid to the account can be counted on when assessing whether an employment takes place under normal pay and employment conditions. This means that you cannot count on board, lodging, diets, free telephony or other services. This is an important element in checking that foreign workers are not employed on paper for a salary similar to that of Danish workers, but in fact end up being much cheaper labour, or that a large part of the wage is made up of benefits of which it is harder to assess the value and which push the wage level for Danish employees, who may not normally be paid with such benefits. In addition, we will make it easier to refuse an application for a work permit if there is a presumption that the actual employment relationship does not correspond to what is stated in the application or that the purpose is to obtain a residence permit which the alien would otherwise not be able to obtain. An application may now be refused if it is found, on a case-by-case basis, that there are specific reasons for believing that the employment offer on which the application is based is not real or that it can be shown in concrete terms that it is pro forma. Unfortunately, however, we have seen that in certain areas there is a risk of circumventing the rules, where the papers appear to be in order, but where the foreigner has not worked in accordance with the application, and we therefore propose that there should no longer be a requirement that certain reasons should be put forward to assume that the employment offer on which the application is based is not real, but that it is sufficient that there is a certain presumption of this. It could, for example, be done. If the company has previously employed foreign labour without the necessary authorisation, or if the wage offered to the worker is, for example: more than 20%. above the median wage for the specific industry, but just above the limit of the amount scheme and this is not justified by the worker's specific qualifications. Then a bell can be dialed, and the amount mechanism can only be used in exceptional cases for posts which are not normally high-paid. Similarly, we will introduce a presumption refusal in the field of internship to ensure that the scheme is used for a real educational purpose and is not abused for social dumping or for easier access to the Danish labour market. We must also prevent underpayment of spouses from the same employer from being part of an informal employment agreement. It should not be possible for a company to bring married couples here, who then work two for the same price, as examples have also been mentioned in e.g. The catering industry, that is, only one person is paid for working in the kitchen, although both actually work for the company and perhaps the other in the dishes. This is totally unacceptable in a Danish labour market. Therefore, we now propose that spouses or other accompanying family members of a foreign worker should have a separate work permit requiring them to work on the usual Danish terms and conditions of employment if they want to work for the same employer or for a company with a close connection with the main person's employer. A close connection can, for example: be in the case of the same physical place of work or where there is a coincidence in the ownership or management of the undertakings. If it is exceeded more than once, the main person's residence permit may be withdrawn according to the new rule, and they must both leave the country. The draft law also provides a legal basis for the Board to carry out independent checks at work in order to ascertain for themselves whether the conditions for foreign workers comply with the conditions laid down in their work permit. In addition, we will allow the board to instruct companies to keep a log of the employees' work and to draw information about crew members on Danish cargo ships in international traffic, and then we will place control attachés on Danish representations abroad, so that control is strengthened already during the application period. We are therefore putting in place new specific rules and we are giving the board a better opportunity to carry out the necessary checks. I am aware that this bill can, of course, give rise to discussion on one element and the other, and we have heard that today. Some believe that we should go even further in the fight against social dumping, while others believe that we are tightening the screw equally well for Danish companies who are just trying to get their daily operations to run with the labour they now need. We have a lot of companies that each day contribute to the Danish economy and to the Danish well-being, not least because of highly trained and qualified labour that they can retrieve abroad, when it cannot be found sufficiently at home, and I would like to stress that in the vast majority of cases it is done completely in accordance with the rules. But there are stingy vessels in between that cheat and that do not want to follow the common rules of the game, and they are the ones that we will try to tighten up with this bill. I think we owe this to the labour market, to companies and to employees who comply with the rules, and this is precisely about establishing the right balance between the two considerations of simultaneously guaranteeing the ability of Danish companies to recruit the necessary labour force and to protect the Danish labour market from social dumping. It is an exercise to find such a balance, and I also think that it has already been shown up to the first reading that there may be a need to adjust the draft law on the requirement for a Danish bank account. I am, for example. have been informed that agricultural practitioners should also be subject to the requirement for a Danish bank account in order to ensure that the internship scheme is used for a real educational purpose and is not abused. I have listened to that, and I agree with it. I am therefore looking at whether an amendment can be tabled so that agricultural practitioners are also included in the demand. On the other hand, I have also been pointed out that this could constitute a special barrier to, for example, the environment. people who are in Denmark on the positive list for a shorter period, that their salary should be paid to a Danish bank account. It can, for example, be done. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to make a few comments on some of the groups that I can hear from some of the rapporteurs here on the platform, in other words that in some cases this may be a problem in relation to, for example, the European Parliament. their family in their country of origin, if the employment is formally to be moved to Denmark for a period of time. Although I believe that it is the right starting point that the salary should be paid to a Danish bank account, nor should we make it so difficult that the highly qualified workforce, which Danish companies have a great need for, chooses Denmark from when it comes to a shorter stay. I have therefore asked my officials also to look at the possibility of a possible amendment which excludes highly qualified and attractive labour from the requirement that wages should be paid to a Danish bank account if they are in Denmark for up to 1⁄2 years instead of the 3 months proposed in the bill. This will apply only to the system known as the positive list, and not to the new skilled employment list, but only to the old one for people with higher education. In those words, I would like to thank the rapporteurs for a good debate. I look forward to the further examination of the bill in the Folketing, and we are, of course, as always ready to answer questions.

I think this is going to be unnecessarily bureaucratic, especially because I actually have a view that the existing model works well, namely that the board is looking at whether we have had similar cases in the past that we can lean on, and if we do not have, we can ask what is called the Regional Labour Councils, where both the trade union movement and the employers' associations are, and then we can hear what they will assess is the normal level of pay. I actually have the impression that it is working quite well. Then there's the thing about the accompanying spouse that I didn't reach, but I might be able to answer that in a follow-up question.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119172323	The debate is open. Sir. Rasmus Stoklund.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119172350	Thank you, President. Many young people from Argentina and Chile have come to Denmark on the Working Holiday scheme in recent years. Often they have been employed by app-based companies; companies with a business model where people associated with companies are self-employed, but on loose and precarious contracts. At the same time, young people themselves have had a responsibility to report their income to tax, and many of them have become in practice a kind of digital day owners. That is to say, no pay during illness, no pension or other rights, as when you are employed on a contract. In the press there are a number of young people from these countries who have expressed wonder that we are now going to tighten up the Working Holiday scheme. And the brief answer to why we want it is that the Working Holiday scheme is not a business scheme. The purpose of the scheme is not that young people from Argentina and Chile and other countries can come to Denmark for the primary purpose of working, the purpose of the Working Holiday scheme is that young people get an opportunity to get to know Denmark and Danish culture, get a good experience and get the opportunity to finance their stay by working along the way. However, among others, 3F expressed concern that the scheme has actually acted as an import of cheap labour from the Third World to the detriment of wage and working conditions in particular. The hotel and catering business and the cleaning business, and we can't live with that. The bill therefore introduces a fee for applications which can be reimbursed if your application is rejected, and then a change in practice will be made, so that people in the Working Holiday scheme can only do wage work in Denmark so that they can no longer be self-employed. In the future, the foreign authorities will also inform young people in the Working Holiday scheme about Danish pay and working conditions, and finally, the bill introduces a quota on the number of residence permits for young people from Argentina and Chile respectively. All in all, we think this is a good bill. It protects the conditions of the Danish labour market, and the Social Democratic Party therefore supports the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119175906	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments. That is why we are now going to the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119175920	Thank you for the floor. I shall of course begin by regretting to the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party that I was not aware earlier in the rapporteur's speech. I would also like to thank you for a good debate, I think, on the legislative proposal. The use of this scheme, the Working Holiday scheme, has increased quite significantly in recent years, so significantly that the number of residence permits issued was actually quadrupled from 2015 to 2019. This development can be attributed to the fact that several young people from Argentina and Chile in particular have made use of the scheme. The purpose of the scheme is to give young people the opportunity to get to know the culture of the other country. With a permit, you get the opportunity to stay for up to 1 year in Denmark, where you can get to know the country and our culture. You are also allowed to work for part of the duration of your stay in order to support your stay financially. The main purpose of the stay must therefore be to take a holiday and any work must be secondary. It is a very special arrangement in which, under relatively relaxed conditions, you can come to Denmark while you are young and have an experience for life. It is therefore important for the government that the use of the scheme works properly and is used for what it is intended for, namely cultural exchange. The government, the Danish People's Party, Enhedslisten and SF reached an agreement last December on a number of initiatives to counter the marked increase we see in the area. The agreement contained four initiatives. Firstly, the introduction of a fee for applications, and that is what we are dealing with with with this bill. Secondly, a change in practice so that it is only allowed to do wage work in Denmark, and not as in the past, where, for example, it is allowed to do wage work in Denmark. was possible to work freelance. This change in practice has been introduced before the summer holidays. Thirdly, the immigration authorities must provide information on the conditions of the Danish labour market in connection with the sending of residence permit letters. It's in progress, too. Fourthly, the introduction of a quota for the number of permits granted to nationals of Argentina and Chile. That dialogue is under way with Argentina and Chile, but it is also part of the legislative proposal we are discussing today. The initiatives already implemented or put into effect are running as planned. Negotiations have therefore been initiated with Argentina and Chile on the introduction of a quota. The bill provides for the introduction of a fee on applications for residence permits under the Working Holiday scheme. This is not unusual in the field of occupational schemes or residence permits, moreover, in the field of foreigners, and it is also being introduced here. In addition, it is proposed to allow the immigration authorities to reject applications submitted after a quota has been used. Finally, it is intended to give the authorities the power to repay the fee for applications rejected because a quota has been used. Finally, I would like to point out that the Working Holiday scheme is currently in place. suspended on the basis of the situation of covid-19. It is therefore not a worthy purpose to come to Denmark on the Working Holiday scheme. In those words, I would like to thank the rapporteurs for a good debate and I look forward to the further consideration of the draft law.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119180734	Thank you, Minister. There are no more brief comments. As no one else has asked to speak, the debate is closed. I propose that the bill be referred to the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Integration. Are there any comments? It's agreed.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119180749	The debate is open. This is the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration. It is the Minister first, because it is a motion for a resolution.
ParlaMint-DK_20201119180819	The Conservative People's Party proposes with the motion for a resolution that the government be required to ensure that priority is given to receiving persecuted Christians and other religious minorities when Denmark welcomes quota refugees. First of all, I would like to acknowledge that Christian minorities in several parts of the world are persecuted and live in extremely difficult conditions. That is clear, among other things. of the Open Doors report referred to in the comments to the proposal. Recently I also read a terrible article about the expulsion of Christians from Iraq. So there is absolutely a relevant issue to address here. In fact, I also believe that I and the author of the motion basically agree that it is important, in connection with the resettlement of quota refugees, to emphasise the ability of those concerned to settle and integrate into Danish society. In my optics a resettlement can normally only be considered successful if the person concerned has the will and ability to become a self-supporting and productive member of Danish society on an equal footing with the other citizens. We must, of course, strive for this with the quota system. But the government can't support this proposal. This is because, in our view, the issue of the reception of quota refugees should also have a strategic objective. The Government therefore considers that the approach described in the proposal may be a disadvantage to the way in which we believe that Denmark makes the most of its participation in international resettlement cooperation. I will come back to that in a moment. In addition, the proposal raises questions in relation to our international obligations. The government wants to pursue a policy in which we can manage more ourselves, how many foreigners come to Denmark, while at the same time we want to take international responsibility. As part of that policy, the government supports aid to the neighbourhoods of refugees and the controlled resettlement of refugees through the UN. In this way, we use our resources best and we help more people in the neighbourhood and spend less money on asylum treatment on Danish soil with challenges with rejected asylum seekers who do not want to go home. It is the government's view that, within the framework of the quota refugee scheme, we must help some of the most vulnerable refugees, as long as we maintain the right balance between the number of refugees that Denmark receives together and the ability of the municipalities to integrate them. In addition, we must ensure that the quota refugees we receive must, as far as possible and within the framework of the quota refugee scheme, be people who have the opportunity to take root in Denmark and benefit from the residence permit as long as the need for protection remains. Under the current rules for the reception of quota refugees, it is up to the Minister of Foreign Affairs to determine annually the number and overall distribution of quota refugees to be repatriated in Denmark. The decision is made on the basis of an overall balance between the social and economic capacity of society, as stated in the preparatory work. In this context, it has, among other things, been able to do so. the importance of how many foreigners have come to Denmark in recent years and how those who are already in Denmark are integrated. As I mentioned earlier, the government believes that the reception of quota refugees should have a strategic aim. This is closely linked to the fact that we have an ambition for a fairer and new asylum system, which means that the resettlement of quota refugees will gradually become the primary way of obtaining protection in Denmark rather than spontaneous asylum. I have asked myself whether we can use resettlement smarter, and I think we can. Resettlement can be used as a strategic tool to promote work on a new and fairer asylum system. For example: We have decided this year that Denmark will receive 200 quota refugees living in refugee camps in Rwanda, Central Africa. We are doing this for the simple reason that earlier this year the government has given financial support to a programme in which Rwanda promises a great deal of responsibility by accepting refugees and asylum seekers from detention centres in Libya, North Africa. Those are the people who, for the most part, intended to cross the Mediterranean and try to join the European Union. That is why I think it makes sense for Denmark to help Rwanda and, in return, to welcome quota refugees from the Democratic Republic of Congo and Burundi who are staying in Rwanda. So it is not those who have been evacuated from Libya that we are relocating in Denmark. We want resettlement in the long term to be the primary route to protection in Denmark. It should not be those who pay the traffickers to embark on a dangerous journey across the Mediterranean to be rewarded with protection in Europe. Rwanda is a good example of a country taking the lead in tackling migration challenges on its own continent. They lift a huge responsibility, and I think we should recognise and back them up in that. The proposal we are debating today also gives me an opportunity to say something about how the selection of quota refugees is carried out. It is the Foreign Affairs Agency which, in close cooperation with the UNHCR, which is the UN Refugee Organisation, makes the specific selection of quota refugees. And the Foreign Affairs Agency is already in dialogue with the UN in relation to the selection of the 200 quota refugees that we have decided to receive in 2020. In the selection process, priority must be given to whether the person concerned can be considered to have a need for protection. Secondly, unless there are particular reasons against it, emphasis must be placed on the possibility for foreigners to take root in Denmark and benefit from the residence permit, including the linguistic prerequisites, educational conditions, work experience, family relationships, networks, age and motivation, all of which are termed integration potential. The actual selection of the quota refugees in question is therefore made on the basis of fairly thorough considerations and interviews. I believe that religion in itself can have an impact on some challenges for integration, but it is simplistic to assess the possibilities of integration on the basis of religion alone. The integration potential must therefore be assessed on the basis of an overall assessment and not on the basis of individual conditions such as religion. Generally speaking, the selection of quota refugees must be based on criteria established in accordance with Denmark's international obligations. This means, among other things, that the criteria must not be contrary to the prohibition of discrimination. This also applies to the aspects of the assessment of integration potential. It follows from the practice of the Court of Human Rights regarding this ban that discrimination on the basis of religious beliefs requires very serious reasons for being justified. There are not immediately considered to be such serious reasons which would justify discrimination against quota refugees solely on the basis of religious beliefs. In general terms, I believe that, within the framework of the quota refugee scheme and our international commitments, we must help some of the most vulnerable refugees, as long as we maintain the right balance between the number of refugees that Denmark receives as a whole and the ability of the municipalities to integrate them. At this stage, I am very concerned about how we can use the quota system smarter as a more strategic tool to promote the work of a new asylum system, as we have already started. In addition, I also believe that the integration potential of quota refugees should be assessed on the basis of an overall assessment and not just on the basis of religious beliefs. That is why the government cannot support the proposal. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20201119185339	Thank you. Our asylum system in Denmark is obviously deeply unfair. A lot of people who need protection around the world can't afford to escape. ParlaMint-DK_20201119191414	Thank you for the floor. I would like to start by saying that I am very pleased that the Conservative People's Party shares my own desire and that of the government to introduce a 37-hour demand. In 2018, the Social Democratic Party presented an alien initiative in which one of the elements was precisely such a 37-hour effort for new arrivals and other benefit recipients who had been in the country for a long time but still had an integration need. As you may have noticed, the government has put forward a proposal to introduce such a 37-hour effort into this year's legislative programme. The starting point for the government is that the Danish welfare society is based on a work ethic, where you go to work and take part in society and contribute actively. That is the clear starting point. The exception to that rule is that there are people who are not in employment and who cannot support themselves and they may receive social benefits under certain conditions. The social contract based on labour ethics must be much more reflected in the demands of new arrivals and other beneficiaries already in the country but still have an integration need. We need more people who lubricate the food package, put on work clothes in the morning, and fewer people who stay at home in the apartment. Indeed, I fully agree with the proposers that employment among immigrants in Denmark is too low. Today, almost half of women from non-western countries are in work. This is, of course, a problem both for women who do not come out into Danish society and experience a normal working day and the community of values that exists among colleagues at a workplace, but also for those children who do not get an opportunity to grow up with an everyday life where Mom and Dad get up in the morning and go to work and come home and tell about today's experiences. The very clear starting point, Plan A, must of course continue to be that you can find a job. If you can't do that as a newcomer, plan B will have to be 37 hours of active action in the future. The same must apply in relation to other beneficiaries of benefits who are in the country but still have an integration need. The 37-hour effort can e.g. In addition, there is a need to ensure that those concerned are outside the home and become an active part of society. Then we must pay for the time and effort that is given, as in a workplace where we are paid for our work. That is to say, if, for example, we are going to do something about it. If you do not show up and do not have legal absence, you will automatically be drawn into your service without too much municipal bureaucracy. Such a model, in which we create a very clear link between effort and remuneration for a particular target group, naturally raises a large number of questions, among others. on how the target group can and must be defined, what the 37 hours of action will consist of, and how the scheme will best operate in an administratively unbureaucratic way in the Danish municipalities. We are currently working in the government to clarify and consider precisely these issues and more. It is a great and complicated work that needs to be done properly, because it will concern many people. Of course, we owe this to the people involved, but we also owe it to society, not least to the municipalities we are asking to administer the scheme and to the labour market, which must also form a major part of the solution. For the same reason, the government cannot simply support this motion for a resolution, even though we are very much in agreement with the thinking behind it, because we are worried that it will leave us with some lines or tracks which are not exactly the right ones. Moreover, the financial costs of the proposal have not yet been clarified. However, I would like to reiterate that I am very pleased with the initiative of the Conservative People's Party. This discussion about the need to meet immigrants with a new logic is important, and I hope, of course, that we can also enter into a dialogue with and get the support of the Conservative People's Party when we come closer to a model that can be dealt with in the Folketing. Thank you for the floor.
